| BUG-242 | **High** / spec P3, AC_SECURITY (an abandoned tab could take the account over) | closed | round-4 hunt (`wf_8f2c40ab-4dd`), fixed in `wf_b0dabd41-8b0` (WEB-R4-AUTH-1) — a defect in the lead's own round-3 fix | Expected: only a session that came from the emailed link may set a password with nothing else, and that grant lasts one change and fifteen minutes (BUG-175). Actual: on the mobile-link path the page ORed its own local `recovery.status === 'ready'` in front of `recoveryActive()`, and that flag is written once by the adopt effect and never reset — so an `/update-password` tab left open after a phone-requested reset still changed the account password with no current password, hours later. | BUG-243 | **High** / AC_SECURITY_02 (a newer safety flag could be hidden for good) | closed | round-4 hunt (`wf_8f2c40ab-4dd`), fixed in `wf_b0dabd41-8b0` (CS-R4-01) — a defect in the lead's own round-3 fix | Expected: a flag waiting on a grown-up is always in the family's list (BUG-188). Actual: the oldest-first bound only drains for rows somebody can resolve, and a parent cannot resolve a report they filed — `PATCH /v1/safety-reports/:id` refuses it, only staff can. 200 open parent reports therefore pinned the page, and a NEW system flag never reached the family at all: worse, for that case, than the newest-first cap it replaced | "An oldest-first bound always drains" — rejected by the round-4 finder: it drains only through an action path, and the lead's own regression test had hidden that by resolving rows with raw SQL while stating that the action path was not under test | The unresolved rows are bounded PER REPORTER KIND: system flags oldest-first (a guardian can address or clear those, so that bound really drains), child-filed and parent-filed reports newest-first in their own branches, plus the newest resolved as before. A reporter kind added later falls into the catch-all branch and is listed rather than silently dropped | `apps/api/tests/privacy-r2.review.test.ts` 'a new flag while the family already filed 200+ reports of its own' → 'lists a newer flag and a newer child report behind 200+ older open parent reports'; the lead's round-3 case was rewritten as 'bounds the unresolved rows per reporter kind, and the flag still drains' and no longer resolves anything by raw SQL; failed before the change | Lesson L-046: a bound drains only through an action path, and a fixture shortcut that bypasses that path proves the property you designed for instead of the one the family needs |
| BUG-244 | **High** / AC_ACCESS_04 (one lost response unpairs a child’s tablet) | **REOPENED — accepted, documented open defect** | round-4 hunt (`wf_8f2c40ab-4dd`) (HUNT4-MOB-1); held open through round 4 by the lead’s decision, then designed and fixed by the lead in the round after it (migration 0880) | Expected: a child keeps working while the pairing is valid. Actual: `/v1/child/refresh` stamps the rotation in the same transaction that produces the response, so a response lost on the way back leaves the tablet holding a token the server has rotated. Its next refresh is read as reuse — the theft signal — and the session is revoked, so one dropped HTTP response unpairs the device and a parent must mint a new pairing code | (a) A 60-second “the response never arrived” grace window, written in the fix round and withdrawn by its own author — REFUSED by the lead: a time window cannot tell the rightful holder’s retry from a blind replay of a stolen token, so it would hand a replayer a live child session in exactly the case it exists to help (lesson L-047). (b) Keying the recovery on the token itself — rejected: a replayer presents exactly that. (c) Re-serving the replacement token that the lost response carried — rejected: only its hash is stored, and storing it in the clear to make that possible would be worse than the bug | The request names itself. `refreshRequestId` (optional, uuid) on `POST /v1/child/refresh`; migration 0880 adds a nullable `private.child_refresh_tokens.used_request_id` with a partial index and no client-role grant; rotation records the id on the row it retires. A presentation of an already-rotated token is recovery rather than theft only when all of: it carries an id AND that id is the one which consumed this token; the replacement that rotation produced is still unused and unexpired (`for update`); the session is live and consent still allows access. Recovery then retires that replacement at the ORIGINAL rotation instant (so it gets no window of its own), issues a fresh token, re-points the chain and logs `child_refresh_recovered` / `LOST_RESPONSE`. Every other presentation still revokes the session, including one with no id, one with a different id, and a replay after the replacement has been used. On the device the id is minted once per refresh, written to secure storage BEFORE the request goes out and cleared only after the rotated token is stored — so an app the OS kills mid-refresh finishes that same refresh on its next cold start; `unpairChildDevice` drops it with the session, and a stored value that is not a uuid is replaced rather than presented forever | `apps/api/tests/mobile-r2.review.test.ts` ‘a refresh whose response was lost is recoverable by its own id (BUG-244)’ — 5 cases: the repro (same token, same id → tokens, not a revocation), a second lost response in a row, a replay with a different id and one with no id at all (both theft), a replay after the replacement was used (theft), and a client that sends no id keeping today’s behaviour exactly. `apps/mobile/src/family/child-session-r2.review.test.ts` ‘a refresh keeps its request id across this device’s own retries (BUG-244)’ — 5 cases incl. the cold start after a process kill and the non-uuid stored value. `supabase/tests/hardening_r1_db2.test.ts` pins the column, the partial index and the absent client grant. Mutation checks: dropping `row.used_request_id === refreshRequestId` from the server guard fails ‘a replay with a different id is theft, and so is one with no id at all’ (200 where 401 is required); dropping the `setItem` of the id fails ‘a process killed mid-refresh finishes that same refresh after a cold start’. Both files restored byte-for-byte (`sha256sum -c`) | Residual, deliberate: a full man-in-the-middle who captures the lost RESPONSE already holds the replacement token, so the id gives such an attacker nothing new; and a device that never retries (uninstall, storage wipe) still needs a new pairing code, which is correct. The parent-side story is unchanged: a genuinely stolen token still ends the session Also deliberate: two presentations of the same token with the same id arriving CONCURRENTLY still end the session — the first retires the replacement under `for update`, so the second finds it used and cannot tell itself from a replay racing the holder. The device single-flights its refresh (`child-session.ts`, L-007), so this needs a client that sends the same attempt twice at once; failing closed there is the right default, and chasing the recovery chain further would trade clarity for a case the client does not produce. SUPERSEDED IN ROUND 5, by its own defects: the hunt over this change found that "the replacement is unclaimed" is NOT "the response was lost" (BUG-248, High), that the retired replacement inherited the request id and was a second recovery key (BUG-249), and that the device kept the id across a session’s end (BUG-286). As shipped at `b802686` the recovery is additionally bounded to two minutes from the rotation — ANDed with the id, never ORed — and writes an audit event, the retired replacement carries no id, and the device drops a stored id older than that window. Read those four rows together: this one records the design, they record what was wrong with it. REOPENED at `5ba16c3`: the recovery that closed this row was REMOVED in round 6 (BUG-294 / HUNT6-A-1). It produced four findings in two days and its recorded residual understated the exposure — a recovery never marked the row it recovered, so one captured request body was served repeatedly for the whole window and each serving returned a full-lifetime rotating refresh token that then rotated on with no id, no window and no audit row, which is a self-renewing child session until the tablet's next refresh, not 'served once'. Migration 0880 was deleted rather than dropped (no deployed database ever had the column). So the ORIGINAL defect stands and is accepted: a lost /v1/child/refresh response unpairs a child's tablet and a parent mints a new pairing code. Owner action #45 is restored. What is NOT accepted, and is why this is the right end state, is the trade the recovery made: a request body captured after its rotation is worthless again, which is the property a logged body needs. |

Actual: the mobile Children screen named a whole-family deletion as if it covered one child (copy fixed by the area's fixer), and the four child routes disagreed about which children a pending deletion covers: PATCH and the `deletionPending` flag tested `scope = 'family' or target_child_id = c.id`, `visibleChild` (activate, pairing-code) tested only the child scope, and archive checked nothing at all — so both clients hid Archive on a deletion-pending child the API would have archived | — | `visibleChild` now carries the same predicate the flag and PATCH use, and `POST /children/:childId/archive` goes through it instead of its own bare select, so all four child routes and both clients agree | `apps/api/tests/family-profile.review.test.ts` 'refuses archiving while the deletion is open, like the other three child routes'; failed before the change (the route answered 200 and archived the child) | Deliberately not done: `deletionPending` still carries no scope, so a client cannot say which scope a request has. That is a contract change and is left for the round that needs it |

| BUG-346 | High / COPPA §312.5 verifiable parental consent, spec P4 child privacy | closed | owner directive "put that into the system that will cover us for Parental consent" | PencilLift recorded NO per-child parental affirmation anywhere. `POST /v1/children` took nickname, gradeLevel and ageBand; `public.child_profiles` had no column naming the adult who said they are that child's parent, what they agreed to, or when. `public.consent_records` covers AI PROCESSING consent per family (provider/method/purpose/policy_version) and is a different claim from parenthood over a named child. The signup order (parent registers, pays, then hands the child a code) makes the parent FIRST in time, which is not the same as having affirmed anything about a particular child, and no verification method verifies parenthood at all — an ID-plus-selfie check establishes ADULTHOOD (a real government ID, a live face matching it), never that this adult is that child's parent. IMPACT: for every child in the product there was no record of who claimed to be their parent, so the company had nothing to produce if asked, and nothing distinguished a parent adding their own child from an adult adding someone else's. | Ruled out before filing: (a) `consent_records` is not it — its rows are per FAMILY and per PROCESSING PURPOSE, with no child column, so it cannot say who affirmed parenthood over which child; (b) parent-first ordering is not consent — verified against the spec's own AC_ACCESS_02, which orders the flow and says nothing about an affirmation; (c) the store receipt carries no age signal — the IAP verification payload was read field by field (16 fields) and holds no birth date, age band or adult flag, so "paid from an adult's Apple ID" is not evidence of adulthood the app can read; (d) a third-party VPC provider (kidentify/skills was supplied and read) would verify adulthood, not parenthood, so it does not remove the need for the affirmation. | Owner decision: confirm the adult, then take a per-child affirmation with the legal statement, then payment, then the child's code. FIX: PREMISE: the affirmation must be un-skippable and its record must be the SERVER's, so the fix works only if (i) no child row can be active without one and (ii) no client can choose the version, the instant or which adult it is recorded against. Both are observable: (i) a constraint on `public.child_profiles`, which `authenticated` reaches directly through the Data API, not only a check in the Hono handler (the BUG-240/L-037 class); (ii) the insert's values list. Migration `0970_parental_attestation.sql` adds attestation_version / attested_at / attested_by (FK auth.users) with `child_profiles_attestation_complete` (all three or none) and `child_profiles_active_requires_attestation` (`status <> 'active' or attested_at is not null`). `packages/contracts/src/family.ts` adds CONSENT_ATTESTATION_VERSION, CONSENT_ATTESTATION_STATEMENT, ATTESTATION_REQUIRED_COPY and `parentalAttestation: z.literal(true)` on the create request (a strictObject, so a client-sent version or instant is refused as an unknown key). `apps/api/src/routes/family.ts` stamps the version from the contract, the instant from `deps.clock()` and the adult from `parent.userId`. Both surfaces collect it in the SAME submission as the child's details, so no child exists un-attested even briefly, and both clear the tick after a success so it never carries to a sibling. | `supabase/tests/consent_attestation.test.ts` 9 cases: an active child with no attestation is refused; an UPDATE to 'active' is refused; an attested active child is allowed; draft and archived are left free; three all-or-none cases; the row records which adult, which version and which instant; archiving keeps the attestation. `apps/api/tests/auth.test.ts` — missing and `false` both 400 VALIDATION_FAILED, extra attestationVersion/attestedAt keys refused, and the stored version is the SERVER's constant with attested_by = the family owner. `apps/mobile/src/family/consent-attestation.test.ts` 9 cases pin the two surfaces as ONE wording and ONE behaviour. MUTATION: six, each verified red and the file restored with `sha256sum -c` — drop parentalAttestation from the phone request; drop the tick-clearing; restore the disabled button on the portal; date the attestation from the request body; reorder the insert's columns; retype the statement instead of importing it. The fourth PASSED at first: the assertion was `expect(api).toMatch(/deps\.clock\(\)/)` and other handlers in the same file call the clock (L-054), so it was re-anchored to the insert's own values list and both then went red. | The affirmation is what the product can honestly claim: it is an ATTESTATION, not verified parenthood. Adult verification (ID + live selfie) is an owner action still outstanding — `docs/Owner_Actions.md` and `docs/design/Consent_Design.md` — and until it is in place the record names a self-asserted adult. |
| BUG-347 | Medium / L-055 one rule two mechanisms, accessibility | closed | this slice, self-review before commit | My own first cut of the attestation checkbox enforced it THREE ways and got two of them wrong. (1) The submit button carried `disabled={busy !== null || !attested}`, so the `if (!attested) setFieldError(...)` branch and ATTESTATION_REQUIRED_COPY were unreachable: the parent got a dead control and no reason. (2) The checkbox's `aria-describedby={fieldError ? attestErrorId : undefined}` pointed at an id NOTHING rendered — the only error paragraph used `errorId` — so a screen reader was sent to a missing element. (3) That one paragraph sits under the Nickname field, so an attestation error would have appeared about 100px above the box it is about. IMPACT: a parent who submits without ticking gets silence on the portal; a screen-reader user gets a dangling reference; and the copy written to explain the refusal could never be shown. | Ruled out before filing: (a) not a lint or typecheck matter — both were clean with all three present, and a dangling aria-describedby is valid TSX; (b) not caught by the existing tests — the add-child cases ticked nothing and asserted nothing about the refusal path, which is why the dead branch survived review. | Root cause: I wrote the guard twice (a disabled control AND a validation branch) and let the weaker one win. FIX: one mechanism — the button stays live and the form validates on submit, reporting BOTH the nickname and the attestation in one pass so fixing one does not earn a second refusal; per-field error slots (`nicknameError`/`attestError`) each with their own rendered id, so both aria references resolve and each message sits at its own field. | `apps/web/src/pages/app/ChildrenPage.test.tsx` gained 'refuses to add a child until the parental attestation is ticked (0970)', which asserts the button is NOT disabled, that the refusal copy shows, that `sends` is empty, and that the id in `aria-describedby` resolves to an element whose text is the refusal — the assertion the dangling id would fail. | BUG-348 | High / spec P4 deletion stops processing, AC_PRIVACY export withdrawal | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-D-1/D-4, fixed and corrected by the lead in this slice | An export could be PUBLISHED after the deletion that was meant to stop it. `childrenBeingDeleted` runs inside the build transaction under READ COMMITTED and BEFORE the file is composed, so a deletion committing after that SELECT left the excluded-children list empty and the deleted child's child_profiles, assignments, extracted_questions (prompt_text, student_answer_text, corrected_*), question_results, attempts, points_ledger, child_feedback and safety_reports rows all went into the file; and for a FAMILY-scope request that check sees nothing at all, because it reads `target_child_id`. The settle's only guard was `status = 'queued'` — the status 0920/0930 had deliberately left standing on the false justification "the builder leaves out every child with an open deletion request (privacy.ts)", repeated in three places — so the row flipped to 'ready' with a live storage_path and a seven-day expiry AFTER the request, and `routes/export-download.ts` (which reads kind, status, storage_path and expires_at and nothing about deletion) minted a signed URL for it. On a child-scope request the family is live, the membership is active and `assertRecentUnlock` is satisfied by the very unlock the deletion required. IMPACT: the parent who asked PencilLift to delete their child's data could download that child's homework for seven days; the window closed only when deletion_purge ran, unbounded if that job dead-lettered. HUNT7-D-4: `app.inactivity_delete_family`, the other writer of a family-scope request, had no `public.data_exports` statement at all, so both residuals lived on that path too. | Ruled out before filing: (a) the job-cancellation statement is not what stops it — a family-wide export_build job carries child_id NULL, so on a child-scope request `child_id = p_child` is NULL and the job is not cancelled, and a job already leased is 'running' and is not cancelled on either scope; (b) the handler's `join public.families f on f.deleted_at is null` covers family scope only and is taken once before the build; (c) the download route resolves nothing about deletion. | Lead: CONFIRMED — and the first fix was too blunt, which is the part worth recording. It made the settle refuse on ANY open deletion request, which reddened a pre-existing spec-P4 case (`tests/export-build.test.ts`: on a child-scope deletion the family-wide file must stay deliverable, carrying the sibling and not the deleted child) — i.e. the fix destroyed an export the family is entitled to, for every parent who had one in flight, and the migration carried the same blunt arm (`or child_id is null`). FIX, corrected: PREMISE: what makes a queued row dangerous is not that a deletion is open but that the BYTES were composed without honouring it, so the only precise test compares the requests open at publish time against the exclusion set the file was actually composed from. That set is observable if it is recorded: `build()` now reads `childrenBeingDeleted` ONCE before any section is composed (it was read separately by `progressRows` and by `familyData`, so two sections of one family_data file could honour different sets) and returns it on `Built.excludedChildren`. The settle refuses when a FAMILY-scope request is open, or a CHILD-scope one whose target is absent from that snapshot; a request the snapshot already honours publishes. Migration 0960 keeps the half it can judge without reading a file — every queued row on a family deletion, the deleted child's own queued exports on a child deletion — and deliberately LEAVES a family-wide queued row for the builder, with the division of labour written into both files. The asymmetry with the 'ready' statement above it (which does take `or child_id is null`) is stated too: a finished file was composed before the request and certainly holds the child's rows. `app.inactivity_delete_family` gets both statements at the caller's STATED clock (p_now), not `now()` — a date comparison on an unstated clock is a defect this project has paid for twice (BUG-090, and this round's job-retention step). The dispatcher's late-removal list is keyed on `status !== 'ready'`, so the row 0960 settles to 'failed' keeps its `private.storage_removals` entry. | `supabase/tests/hardening_r5_db.test.ts` runs the builder's own settle statement as the job would run it a moment after the deletion committed, and asserts it returns no row for the deleted child's export while the family-wide row stays 'queued' for the builder. `apps/api/tests/privacy-r2.review.test.ts` covers both halves: the race (compose, deletion, settle → refused, 409 on download, uploaded bytes removed) and the division of labour (the child's own row 'failed' by 0960, the family-wide row left 'queued', and the purge scheduling a late storage pass for BOTH — the 'failed' one is exactly what `status === 'queued'` would have missed). `tests/export-build.test.ts`'s spec-P4 case passes unchanged, which is the evidence the correction needed. MUTATION: four, each red and each file restored with `sha256sum -c` — drop the settle's deletion check (the original defect); re-read the deletion set at settle instead of carrying the snapshot (the READ COMMITTED race — 8 cases red); give 0960 the broad arm back; key the late-removal list on `status === 'queued'`. | The family-scope arm of the 'ready' withdrawal is kept as defence in depth, not because a tombstoned family's adult can reach the download — `export-download.ts` resolves `currentFamilyId` from an ACTIVE membership before it reads `public.data_exports`, and both family-scope writers revoke those memberships in the same transaction. 0920/0930's claim that the arm was load-bearing is corrected in those files. |
| BUG-349 | Low / CLAUDE.md "child writes go through API-owned SECURITY DEFINER functions" | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-D-3 | The `pl_child` write invariant's column arm had an unplanted privilege: the column-level INSERT its own lead example writes out in full (`grant insert (family_id, child_id) on public.<family table> to pl_child`). The query has four independent lists — two grantee lists and two privilege lists — and round 6 pinned all four GRANTEE cells with five plants, then said in the comment that "no arm can be dropped". Both column-level plants were UPDATE, so `'INSERT'` could be deleted from the column arm's privilege list and all five plants stayed green: plant 1 survives because the TABLE arm still names PUBLIC and INSERT, and `information_schema.role_column_grants` expands a table-level grant to every column. IMPACT: no live hole — the invariant does catch such a grant today — but the one arm a future narrowing edit could delete with CI green was the arm protecting family tables from a paired child's direct INSERT. HUNT6-E-4 with one word moved: there the deletable word was a grantee, here a privilege. | Ruled out before filing: the hunter verified all four grantee mutations still red (dropping 'PUBLIC' from the table arm reds plant 2, 'pl_child' from it reds plant 5, 'PUBLIC' from the column arm reds plant 4, 'pl_child' from it reds plant 3), so this is not a re-file of E-4; and checked TRUNCATE, which also has no plant here, is covered by the '[DB-R2-04]' case. | Lead: CONFIRMED. Plant it, so the arm can fail. FIX: plant 6 is `grant insert (metadata) on public.audit_events to pl_child`, a column-level INSERT no table-level view reports, so only the column arm's `'INSERT'` can see it. AND — found while proving the fix — the TABLE arm's `'INSERT'` and `'UPDATE'` can BOTH be deleted with every plant green, which is redundancy rather than a hole: the column view expands a table-level INSERT/UPDATE to every column, so the column arm reports those grants too, and plant 1 (a table-level insert) surviving that deletion is the demonstration. The words only the table arm can report are DELETE and TRUNCATE, and both are planted. That is written into the comment, so every word in the query is now either pinned by a plant or provably redundant and the next reader who mutates one knows which. | `supabase/tests/schema_invariants.test.ts` 10 cases. MUTATION: deleting `'INSERT'` from the column arm reds plant 6 and nothing else (verified; restored with `sha256sum -c`); deleting it from the table arm stays green, which is the redundancy now documented rather than a second finding. | The redundancy is left in place: the table arm's privilege list reads as the complete set of write privileges, which is what a reader checks it against, and narrowing it to DELETE/TRUNCATE would make the query harder to read for no gain. |
| BUG-350 | Low / L-053 when code and prose disagree, decide which is TRUE | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-D-2 | Migration 0930 defines "the canonical order" for this codebase and then claimed the archive route, the billing writers and the purges "now agree with this function statement for statement". False in three places: `public.request_deletion` revokes `child_sessions` and `child_devices` BEFORE the {child_slot_assignments, child_profiles} pair while the archive route writes them AFTER it, so for {child_profiles, child_sessions} and {child_slot_assignments, child_sessions} the two are inverted; `app.purge_family_data` deletes the assignment at a different point; and `public.data_exports` is not written by the archive route at all. IMPACT: no deadlock is reachable, because every one of these writers serialises on the family row first — but 0930 is the file the next author reads, and it told them the sequences agree, so they would conclude that copying this function's order is what keeps a new writer safe. It is not. | Ruled out before filing: the hunter enumerated every writer of `child_profiles` and `child_slot_assignments` (activate, archive, PATCH profile, create child, the pairing-code route, billing's applyCapacity/releaseSlotlessProfiles under all three FOR UPDATE callers, `app.purge_family_data`, `app.inactivity_delete_family`, `app.enforce_slot_capacity`) to confirm the SECOND clause — no cycle among the pair's writers — is sound, so only the first clause is disputed. | Lead: CONFIRMED as prose. Say which differ and why it is safe; do not leave the claim standing. FIX: 0930's comment now leads with the claim that carries the weight — every writer of this pair takes the family row first, by FOR UPDATE or by the KEY SHARE the `deletion_requests` foreign key needs — keeps the agreement that is true (assignment, then profile) and names the three disagreements explicitly, with the conclusion a reader needs: a new writer takes the family row first or re-derives this argument from scratch. My first draft of the correction cited four line numbers and three were already stale (my own edit had shifted 0930's), so it cites STATEMENTS instead and says why. | `supabase/tests/hardening_r5_db.test.ts` pins the claim in BOTH directions, because a comment asserting a disagreement can rot the other way: the function's order is read from `pg_get_functiondef` and asserted sessions → devices → assignment → profile; the archive route's is read from source and asserted assignment → profile → sessions → devices; and 0930 is asserted to carry the corrected text and not the false sentence. The family-row-first claim itself was already proven by the real interleaving case in the same file. MUTATION: two, both red and both restored with `sha256sum -c` — put 0930's false sentence back; reorder the archive route to write sessions before the pair. | — |
| BUG-351 | Medium / spend control, owner cost visibility | closed | round-7 hunt HUNT7-B-2 | `docs/Cost_Analysis.md`'s worst-case-spend-HOLD table omitted the Thursday review's personalization hold, and the paragraph under it named 483,240 micro-USD ($0.48) as "the largest single hold a family can hit". The largest is 933,600 ($0.93), 93% more. Not a hypothetical path: `personalizeItems` takes its hold as `acquireSpendHold(deps, PROPOSED_STAGE_COST_BUDGET_MICROS[stage])` with `stage: 'daily_set' | 'thursday_bundle'`; `generateThursdayReview` is registered as the `thursday_review_generate` handler, is enqueued by the scheduled tick, and calls `personalizeItems` with `'thursday_bundle'`; and `PROPOSED_STAGE_COST_BUDGET_MICROS.thursday_bundle` is 933,600. IMPACT: the document the owner reads to decide what PencilLift may spend understated its biggest single reservation by almost half, so a spend ceiling set from that table would have been set too low and the Thursday job would have been refused a hold it needs. | Ruled out before filing: the figure is present in the STAGE table two tables up (`| thursday_bundle | 700,000 | 933,600 | 933,600 |`), so this is a hold-table omission and a false sentence, not a wrong constant — the code is right and the prose is wrong (L-053). | Lead: CONFIRMED. FIX: the hold table gains the Thursday row, the paragraph names 933,600 as the largest with the call path that reaches it, and — because a hand-maintained table of constants drifts invisibly, which is exactly how this row went missing — `packages/ai/src/cost-doc.test.ts` now GENERATES every assertion from `packages/ai/src/routing.ts`: each stage's admission cap, budget and full-raise ceiling must appear in the stage table's row for that stage, each single-stage hold must appear in the hold table, and the "largest single hold" sentence must carry the largest figure and must not name a smaller one. | `packages/ai/src/cost-doc.test.ts` 3 cases. MUTATION: five, each restored with `sha256sum -c` — delete the Thursday hold row; restore the understated sentence; change `thursday_bundle`'s budget without touching the doc; change coaching's admission cap without touching the doc; delete a stage-table row. THE FIRST ONE PASSED AT FIRST, which is the part worth recording: the hold-table search was bounded to "any table line containing the stage name and the figure", and after deleting the hold row the STAGE table's own row satisfied it — the defect the test exists to catch, passing. The search is now bounded to the one table with the header `| Hold | Stages summed | Micro-USD | USD |` and all five mutations red (L-054). | The two tables stay hand-written and are checked against the code rather than generated into the document: the prose around them explains WHY each number is what it is, which a generator cannot write. |
